========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, what finally pushed me to make the appointment was this thing I keep doing where I just… get up when I’m supposed to stay seated. It sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it’s been bothering me.

Like during sprint planning at work, I’ll know I need to sit there and listen, and then suddenly I’m up getting water, or I decide I need to check something in the other room, or I’ll just sort of wander off for a minute. And it’s not like I don’t understand that I should stay put — I do. Same thing has happened during mortgage calls, which is especially embarrassing because those feel important and adult and I’m trying to be professional, but my body just kind of decides we’re moving now.

It’s mild, I guess, in the sense that I’m not causing some huge disaster, but it’s frequent enough that I started thinking, okay, maybe this is something I should actually talk to someone about instead of just telling myself to stop doing it.

Yes. Yeah, that’s actually a really good way to put it. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think — like I can be sitting there looking normal, maybe even kind of calm — but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building, like I need to move or change what I’m doing right now.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally pushed me to make the appointment was this thing I keep doing where I just… get up when I’m supposed to stay seated. It sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nLike during sprint planning at work, I’ll know I need to sit there and listen, and then suddenly I’m up getting water, or I decide I need to check something in the other room, or I’ll just sort of wander off for a minute. And it’s not like I don’t understand that I should stay put — I do. Same thing has happened during mortgage calls, which is especially embarrassing because those feel important and adult and I’m trying to be professional, but my body just kind of decides we’re moving now.\n\nIt’s mild, I guess, in the sense that I’m not causing some huge disaster, but it’s frequent enough that I started thinking, okay, maybe this is something I should actually talk to someone about instead of just telling myself to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel a strong inner restlessness in your body that makes sitting still hard?\n\nPatient: Yes. Same thing has happened during mortgage calls, which is especially embarrassing because those feel important and adult and I’m trying to be professional, but my body just kind of decides we’re moving now.\n\nIt’s mild, I guess, in the sense that I’m not causing some huge disaster, but it’s frequent enough that I started thinking, okay, maybe this is something I should actually talk to someone about instead of just telling myself to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel a strong inner restlessness in your body that makes sitting still hard?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, that’s actually a really good way to put it. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think — like I can be sitting there looking normal, maybe even kind of calm — but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building, like I need to move or change what I’m doing right now.\n\nIt’s almost like my body is saying, “Get up, switch tasks, do something with your hands, anything,” and if I try to just sit through it, I get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Not in a dramatic way every single time, but enough that sitting still starts to feel like effort instead of just… sitting.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after you began taking a medication or after a dose was changed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you a sloppy answer, but I haven’t noticed it lining up with starting a medication or changing a dose.\n\nIt feels more like… this has just been part of how I am in situations where I’m expected to sit still, rather than something that suddenly appeared after a medication change. But if you mean any medication at all, I might need to go back and look at dates to be totally sure. Nothing jumps out to me as, “Oh, this started right after that.”\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you get up, does it feel like you have to do it to relieve a specific thought or fear?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. At least, I don’t think so.

It doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t get up, something bad will happen,” or like I’m trying to neutralize a specific fear. It’s not tied to a particular thought like needing to check the door or prevent something. It’s more vague than that — more like my body is uncomfortable being still, and getting up gives me a little release from that tension.

Sometimes I’ll make up a reason after the fact, like, “Oh, I should refill my water,” or “I’ll just grab that thing,” but it doesn’t feel driven by one scary thought. It’s more like an urge to move than a fear I’m trying to fix.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. I mean, obviously I’m remembering it through kid-memory and also through things my parents and teachers said, but yeah — this goes pretty far back.

In elementary school, I remember teachers moving my desk sometimes, like putting me closer to them or away from certain other kids, because I was always popping up or turning around or talking when I wasn’t supposed to. Not always in a malicious way — I wasn’t trying to be defiant, I don’t think — I just had this thing where if I had a thought, it came out, even if I hadn’t been called on.

And I got reminded to stay seated a lot. Like, “Sit down,” “Raise your hand,” “Wait your turn,” that kind of thing. I also remember rushing through assignments, even when I understood the work. I’d just want to be done, or move on, or answer quickly, and then I’d make careless mistakes or finish way before I was supposed to. So yes, I wouldn’t say it suddenly started as an adult. It was definitely there when I was little too.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do. It’s not every single minute, and if something is very urgent or really interesting I can sometimes lock in for a while, but a lot of the time I have trouble staying with something even when I genuinely intend to.

Like at work, I’ll open a pull request to review and I’m telling myself, “Okay, read this carefully, don’t skim, actually understand what changed.” And I’ll start out fine, but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same block of code or the same comment and I’m not really processing it anymore. My eyes are on it, but my brain has wandered off into, I don’t know, five unrelated things — something I need to do later, an email I forgot to answer, a random idea, whatever.

It happens with non-work things too. We were reading mortgage documents, and I really wanted to focus because obviously that’s important, but I kept having to reread the same section. I’d get halfway through a paragraph and then notice I had no idea what it said. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I’ll be trying to pay attention, and then I sort of come to and realize I’ve drifted without meaning to.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n6\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.9, F41.9, F95.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Honestly, what finally pushed me to make the appointment was this thing I keep doing where I just… get up when I’m supposed to stay seated. It sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nLike during sprint planning at work, I’ll know I need to sit there and listen, and then suddenly I’m up getting water, or I decide I need to check something in the other room, or I’ll just sort of wander off for a minute. And it’s not like I don’t understand that I should stay put — I do. Same thing has happened during mortgage calls, which is especially embarrassing because those feel important and adult and I’m trying to be professional, but my body just kind of decides we’re moving now.\n\nIt’s mild, I guess, in the sense that I’m not causing some huge disaster, but it’s frequent enough that I started thinking, okay, maybe this is something I should actually talk to someone about instead of just telling myself to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel a strong inner restlessness in your body that makes sitting still hard?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, that’s actually a really good way to put it. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think — like I can be sitting there looking normal, maybe even kind of calm — but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building, like I need to move or change what I’m doing right now.\n\nIt’s almost like my body is saying, “Get up, switch tasks, do something with your hands, anything,” and if I try to just sit through it, I get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Not in a dramatic way every single time, but enough that sitting still starts to feel like effort instead of just… sitting.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after you began taking a medication or after a dose was changed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you a sloppy answer, but I haven’t noticed it lining up with starting a medication or changing a dose.\n\nIt feels more like… this has just been part of how I am in situations where I’m expected to sit still, rather than something that suddenly appeared after a medication change. But if you mean any medication at all, I might need to go back and look at dates to be totally sure. Nothing jumps out to me as, “Oh, this started right after that.”\n\nDoctor: When you get up, does it feel like you have to do it to relieve a specific thought or fear?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t get up, something bad will happen,” or like I’m trying to neutralize a specific fear. It’s not tied to a particular thought like needing to check the door or prevent something. It’s more vague than that — more like my body is uncomfortable being still, and getting up gives me a little release from that tension.\n\nSometimes I’ll make up a reason after the fact, like, “Oh, I should refill my water,” or “I’ll just grab that thing,” but it doesn’t feel driven by one scary thought. It’s more like an urge to move than a fear I’m trying to fix.\n\nDoctor: Were you like this as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, obviously I’m remembering it through kid-memory and also through things my parents and teachers said, but yeah — this goes pretty far back.\n\nIn elementary school, I remember teachers moving my desk sometimes, like putting me closer to them or away from certain other kids, because I was always popping up or turning around or talking when I wasn’t supposed to. Not always in a malicious way — I wasn’t trying to be defiant, I don’t think — I just had this thing where if I had a thought, it came out, even if I hadn’t been called on.\n\nAnd I got reminded to stay seated a lot. Like, “Sit down,” “Raise your hand,” “Wait your turn,” that kind of thing. I also remember rushing through assignments, even when I understood the work. I’d just want to be done, or move on, or answer quickly, and then I’d make careless mistakes or finish way before I was supposed to. So yes, I wouldn’t say it suddenly started as an adult. It was definitely there when I was little too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining attention on tasks or conversations, even when you want to focus?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not every single minute, and if something is very urgent or really interesting I can sometimes lock in for a while, but a lot of the time I have trouble staying with something even when I genuinely intend to.\n\nLike at work, I’ll open a pull request to review and I’m telling myself, “Okay, read this carefully, don’t skim, actually understand what changed.” And I’ll start out fine, but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same block of code or the same comment and I’m not really processing it anymore. My eyes are on it, but my brain has wandered off into, I don’t know, five unrelated things — something I need to do later, an email I forgot to answer, a random idea, whatever.\n\nIt happens with non-work things too. We were reading mortgage documents, and I really wanted to focus because obviously that’s important, but I kept having to reread the same section. I’d get halfway through a paragraph and then notice I had no idea what it said. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I do, though I’ve gotten kind of used to working around it, so sometimes I don’t notice how messy my system is until I’m already behind.

It’s like… I’ll have work tickets in our project tracker, but then some important detail about the ticket is in Slack because someone mentioned it in a thread. Then I’ll write myself a sticky note so I don’t forget the Slack thing, but then I’ll also email myself something or put a reminder in my phone. And after a while it’s not one system, it’s like four half-systems that don’t really talk to each other.

Same at home. If there’s a repair thing, like something with the house, I might have notes in my phone, a contractor’s email in my inbox, a random piece of paper on the counter, and then a budget reminder somewhere else. So when I finally sit down to do the task, I spend this ridiculous amount of time just trying to reconstruct what I was supposed to do. Like, “Where did I put that estimate?” or “Did I already reply to that message?” or “Was this the thing I was supposed to call about?”

And it’s embarrassing because it’s not that the task itself is always hard. Sometimes the actual thing would take ten minutes, but finding the information and figuring out what the next step was takes longer than doing it. So yes, organizing tasks and keeping the pieces together is definitely a problem for me.

It sounds kind of small when I say it out loud, but it’s been bothering me.\n\nLike during sprint planning at work, I’ll know I need to sit there and listen, and then suddenly I’m up getting water, or I decide I need to check something in the other room, or I’ll just sort of wander off for a minute. And it’s not like I don’t understand that I should stay put — I do. Same thing has happened during mortgage calls, which is especially embarrassing because those feel important and adult and I’m trying to be professional, but my body just kind of decides we’re moving now.\n\nIt’s mild, I guess, in the sense that I’m not causing some huge disaster, but it’s frequent enough that I started thinking, okay, maybe this is something I should actually talk to someone about instead of just telling myself to stop doing it.\n\nDoctor: When this happens, do you feel a strong inner restlessness in your body that makes sitting still hard?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yeah, that’s actually a really good way to put it. It’s not always obvious from the outside, I don’t think — like I can be sitting there looking normal, maybe even kind of calm — but inside it feels like there’s this pressure building, like I need to move or change what I’m doing right now.\n\nIt’s almost like my body is saying, “Get up, switch tasks, do something with your hands, anything,” and if I try to just sit through it, I get this really uncomfortable feeling, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin. Not in a dramatic way every single time, but enough that sitting still starts to feel like effort instead of just… sitting.\n\nDoctor: Did this start after you began taking a medication or after a dose was changed?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I’m trying to think back carefully, because I don’t want to give you a sloppy answer, but I haven’t noticed it lining up with starting a medication or changing a dose.\n\nIt feels more like… this has just been part of how I am in situations where I’m expected to sit still, rather than something that suddenly appeared after a medication change. But if you mean any medication at all, I might need to go back and look at dates to be totally sure. Nothing jumps out to me as, “Oh, this started right after that.”\n\nDoctor: When you get up, does it feel like you have to do it to relieve a specific thought or fear?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t get up, something bad will happen,” or like I’m trying to neutralize a specific fear. It’s not tied to a particular thought like needing to check the door or prevent something. It’s more vague than that — more like my body is uncomfortable being still, and getting up gives me a little release from that tension.\n\nSometimes I’ll make up a reason after the fact, like, “Oh, I should refill my water,” or “I’ll just grab that thing,” but it doesn’t feel driven by one scary thought. It’s more like an urge to move than a fear I’m trying to fix.\n\nDoctor: Were you like this as a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, obviously I’m remembering it through kid-memory and also through things my parents and teachers said, but yeah — this goes pretty far back.\n\nIn elementary school, I remember teachers moving my desk sometimes, like putting me closer to them or away from certain other kids, because I was always popping up or turning around or talking when I wasn’t supposed to. Not always in a malicious way — I wasn’t trying to be defiant, I don’t think — I just had this thing where if I had a thought, it came out, even if I hadn’t been called on.\n\nAnd I got reminded to stay seated a lot. Like, “Sit down,” “Raise your hand,” “Wait your turn,” that kind of thing. I also remember rushing through assignments, even when I understood the work. I’d just want to be done, or move on, or answer quickly, and then I’d make careless mistakes or finish way before I was supposed to. So yes, I wouldn’t say it suddenly started as an adult. It was definitely there when I was little too.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble sustaining attention on tasks or conversations, even when you want to focus?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not every single minute, and if something is very urgent or really interesting I can sometimes lock in for a while, but a lot of the time I have trouble staying with something even when I genuinely intend to.\n\nLike at work, I’ll open a pull request to review and I’m telling myself, “Okay, read this carefully, don’t skim, actually understand what changed.” And I’ll start out fine, but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same block of code or the same comment and I’m not really processing it anymore. My eyes are on it, but my brain has wandered off into, I don’t know, five unrelated things — something I need to do later, an email I forgot to answer, a random idea, whatever.\n\nIt happens with non-work things too. We were reading mortgage documents, and I really wanted to focus because obviously that’s important, but I kept having to reread the same section. I’d get halfway through a paragraph and then notice I had no idea what it said. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I’ll be trying to pay attention, and then I sort of come to and realize I’ve drifted without meaning to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks or activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do, though I’ve gotten kind of used to working around it, so sometimes I don’t notice how messy my system is until I’m already behind.\n\nIt’s like… I’ll have work tickets in our project tracker, but then some important detail about the ticket is in Slack because someone mentioned it in a thread. Then I’ll write myself a sticky note so I don’t forget the Slack thing, but then I’ll also email myself something or put a reminder in my phone. And after a while it’s not one system, it’s like four half-systems that don’t really talk to each other.\n\nSame at home. If there’s a repair thing, like something with the house, I might have notes in my phone, a contractor’s email in my inbox, a random piece of paper on the counter, and then a budget reminder somewhere else. So when I finally sit down to do the task, I spend this ridiculous amount of time just trying to reconstruct what I was supposed to do. Like, “Where did I put that estimate?” or “Did I already reply to that message?” or “Was this the thing I was supposed to call about?”\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing because it’s not that the task itself is always hard. Sometimes the actual thing would take ten minutes, but finding the information and figuring out what the next step was takes longer than doing it. So yes, organizing tasks and keeping the pieces together is definitely a problem for me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

Symptoms are not medication-linked, not driven by obsessions/fears, and are better explained than anxiety or tic disorder, so F41.9 and F95.9 are ruled out.","is_final":true}

Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S011\",\"name\":\"Leaving_Seat\",\"description\":\"Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\",\"reason\":\"This is the first reply of the consultation, so I’m returning the chief complaint symptom code.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.   }

Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation. Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"None\",\"name\":\"None\",\"description\":\"None\",\"reason\":\"The doctor is asking whether the symptoms began after starting or changing a medication, but the provided symptom profile does not include medication use, medication changes, or substance/medical causes.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nNot really, no. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nIt doesn’t feel like, “If I don’t get up, something bad will happen,” or like I’m trying to neutralize a specific fear. It’s not tied to a particular thought like needing to check the door or prevent something. It’s more vague than that — more like my body is uncomfortable being still, and getting up gives me a little release from that tension.\n\nSometimes I’ll make up a reason after the fact, like, “Oh, I should refill my water,” or “I’ll just grab that thing,” but it doesn’t feel driven by one scary thought. It’s more like an urge to move than a fear I’m trying to fix.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes, I do. It’s not every single minute, and if something is very urgent or really interesting I can sometimes lock in for a while, but a lot of the time I have trouble staying with something even when I genuinely intend to.\n\nLike at work, I’ll open a pull request to review and I’m telling myself, “Okay, read this carefully, don’t skim, actually understand what changed.” And I’ll start out fine, but after a few minutes I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same block of code or the same comment and I’m not really processing it anymore. My eyes are on it, but my brain has wandered off into, I don’t know, five unrelated things — something I need to do later, an email I forgot to answer, a random idea, whatever.\n\nIt happens with non-work things too. We were reading mortgage documents, and I really wanted to focus because obviously that’s important, but I kept having to reread the same section. I’d get halfway through a paragraph and then notice I had no idea what it said. It’s frustrating because it’s not that I don’t care. I’ll be trying to pay attention, and then I sort of come to and realize I’ve drifted without meaning to.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
